A De Giorgi conjecture on the regularity of minimizers of Cartesian area in 1D
Authors: Giovanni Bellettini, Shokhrukh Yu Kholmatov
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
We prove a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{1,1}$$\end{document}C1,1-regularity result for minimizers of the functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \int _I \sqrt{1+|Du|^2} + \int _I |u-g|ds,\quad u\in BV(I), $$\end{document}∫I1+|Du|2+∫I|u-g|ds,u∈BV(I),provided \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I\subset \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}I⊂R is a bounded open interval and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert g\Vert _\infty $$\end{document}‖g‖∞ is sufficiently small, thus partially establishing a De Giorgi conjecture in dimension one and codimension one. We also extend our result to a suitable anisotropic setting.

Introduction

The non-parametric minimal surfaces, more generally, the prescribed mean curvature surfaces, have been extensively studied in the literature from the variational perspective (see e.g. [11, 14, 16–18] and the references therein). Given an open set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega \subset \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}Ω⊂Rn and a sufficiently regular function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H:\Omega \rightarrow \mathbb {R},$$\end{document}H:Ω→R, the underlying equation is rewritten as1.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{div}\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^2}} = H\quad \text {in } \Omega \end{aligned}$$\end{document}div∇u1+|∇u|2=HinΩwith a prescribed Dirichlet or Neumann boundary condition, and corresponds to the Euler-Lagrange equation of the functional1.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _\Omega \sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^2}dx + \int _\Omega Hu\,dx,\quad u\in C^1(\Omega ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫Ω1+|∇u|2dx+∫ΩHudx,u∈C1(Ω).It is well-known that under suitable assumptions on Ω Ω and H, the minimizers are in fact locally \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{2+\alpha },$$\end{document}C2+α, and hence solve (1.1) in a classical sense.

In the context of functionals with linear growth, a related problem is the existence and regularity of minimizers of the (convex, but not strictly convex) functional\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathscr {F}(u):=\int _\Omega \sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^2}dx + \int _\Omega |u-g|\,dx,\quad u\in C^1(\Omega ), $$\end{document}F(u):=∫Ω1+|∇u|2dx+∫Ω|u-g|dx,u∈C1(Ω),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g\in L^1(\Omega )$$\end{document}g∈L1(Ω) is given, see [7]. In this case, the associated Euler-Lagrange equation becomes formally a differential inclusion of the form1.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{div}\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^2}} \in {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \{1\} & \text {in } \{u>g\},\\ {[}-1,1] & \text {in }\{u=g\}, \\ \{-1\} & \text {in } \{u<g\}, \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}div∇u1+|∇u|2∈{1}in{u>g},[-1,1]in{u=g},{-1}in{u<g},thus, in the sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u>g\}$$\end{document}{u>g} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u<g\},$$\end{document}{u<g}, the subgraph of u has mean curvature equal to 1 and -1,-1, respectively.

Unlike the minimizers of the functional in (1.2), the equation (1.3) may admit nonregular solutions, as observed in [7]. For instance, if n=1,n=1, Ω =(-1,1)Ω=(-1,1) and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ g(s) = {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} 2 & \text {if } s\in (0,1),\\ -2 & \text {if } s\in (-1,0), \end{array}\right. } $$\end{document}g(s)=2ifs∈(0,1),-2ifs∈(-1,0),one can readily check that the functions1.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u_{a,b}(s) = {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \sqrt{2s-s^2} + a & \text {if } s\in (0,1),\\ \sqrt{-2s-s^2} + b & \text {if } s\in (-1,0) \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ua,b(s)=2s-s2+aifs∈(0,1),-2s-s2+bifs∈(-1,0)with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-1\le b\le a\le 1$$\end{document}-1≤b≤a≤1 satisfy (1.3) and minimize \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {F},$$\end{document}F, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {F}(u_{a,b})=4+\frac{\pi }{2}.$$\end{document}F(ua,b)=4+π2. However, any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{a,b}$$\end{document}ua,b is not continuously differentiable at s=0,s=0, worse still, it has a jump if a>b.a>b.

To study regularity of minimizers of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {F},$$\end{document}F, in [7] De Giorgi posed the following conjecture, which seems nontrivial even when n=k=1.n=k=1.

Conjecture 1.1

For any n,k≥ 1,n,k≥1, there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma :=\sigma (n,k)>0$$\end{document}σ:=σ(n,k)>0 such that for any open ball \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g\in L^\infty (B;\mathbb {R}^k)$$\end{document}g∈L∞(B;Rk) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \begin{document}$$\Vert g\Vert _\infty \le \sigma $$\end{document}‖g‖∞≤σ the following minimum is achieved:1.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \min \Big \{\int _B \sqrt{1+\sum |M_i(\nabla u)|^2} dx + \int _B |u-g|dx:\,\,u\in C^1(B;\mathbb {R}^k)\Big \}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}min{∫B1+∑|Mi(∇u)|2dx+∫B|u-g|dx:u∈C1(B;Rk)},where the sum is taken over all minors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} A variation of this conjecture for n=1n=1 and k≥ 1k≥1 has been recently addressed in [5]: using the Sobolev regularity theory for the minimizers of an Ambrosio-Tortorelli-type functional [2], the authors have shown the existence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma :=\sigma (k,|I|^{-1/2})>0,$$\end{document}σ:=σ(k,|I|-1/2)>0, such that for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g\in L^\infty (I;\mathbb {R}^k)$$\end{document}g∈L∞(I;Rk) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert g\Vert _\infty \le \sigma ,$$\end{document}‖g‖∞≤σ, the minimum problem1.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \min \Big \{\int _I \sqrt{1+|u'|^2}\,ds+ \int _I (u-g)^2\,ds:\,\, u\in C^1(I;\mathbb {R}^k)\Big \} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}min{∫I1+|u′|2ds+∫I(u-g)2ds:u∈C1(I;Rk)}admits a unique solution, where I is a bounded interval. This result does not solve Conjecture 1.1, due to the exponent 2 in the second integral of the functional and to the dependence of σ σ on the length |I| of the interval I. To prove the existence of solutions, they observe that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in W^{1,\infty }(I;\mathbb {R}^k)$$\end{document}u∈W1,∞(I;Rk) minimizes the Γ Γ-limit F of a suitable sequence of approximating functionals, then it also minimizes the functional in (1.6), which turns out to be Sobolev regular provided that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert g\Vert _\infty $$\end{document}‖g‖∞ is small enough depending only on |I|. Next, they show that u is in fact a solution to the corresponding Euler-Lagrange equation with suitable boundary conditions, which yield the continuity of the derivative (here the quadratic term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u-g)^2$$\end{document}(u-g)2 is important in the analysis of the Euler-Lagrange equation).

The main result of this paper reads as follows (see also Theorem 5.4).

Theorem 1.2

Let φ φ be an anisotropy on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}R2 such that the unit ball \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^\varphi :=\{\varphi \le 1\}$$\end{document}Wφ:={φ≤1} is symmetric with respect to the coordinate axes and does not have vertical facets. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I\subset \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}I⊂R be a bounded open interval. Then there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma :=\sigma (\varphi ,p,|I|)>0$$\end{document}σ:=σ(φ,p,|I|)>0 such that for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g\in L^\infty (I)$$\end{document}g∈L∞(I) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert g\Vert _\infty < \sigma $$\end{document}‖g‖∞<σ every minimizer of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {G}$$\end{document}G is Lipschitz continuous in I. Additionally, if φ φ is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2 away from the origin and elliptic (see Definition 2.1), then minimizers are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{1,1}$$\end{document}C1,1 in I.

In the Euclidean case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi =|\cdot |,$$\end{document}φ=|·|, Theorem 1.2 provides a positive solution to Conjecture 1.1 for n=k=1,n=k=1, except that our σ σ depends on |I| (as in [5]); at the same time we gain an extra regularity of minimizers.

To prove Theorem 1.2, we begin by observing that if g is bounded, then every minimizer u of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {G}$$\end{document}G is also bounded, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert u\Vert _\infty \le \Vert g\Vert _\infty $$\end{document}‖u‖∞≤‖g‖∞ (see Lemma 5.1). If, additionally, φ φ is even in each coordinate (equivalently, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^\varphi $$\end{document}Wφ is symmetric with respect to the coordinate axes), then the subgraph and the epigraph of u are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\gamma ,\Lambda )$$\end{document}(γ,Λ)-local minimizers of the φ φ-perimeter, in the sense of Definition 4.5 below, with suitable constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma ,\Lambda $$\end{document}γ,Λ (Proposition 5.3). Next in Proposition 4.3, we prove that if u satisfies an appropriate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^\infty $$\end{document}L∞-bound depending only on Λ Λ and |I|, a tangent φ φ-ball condition holds at each point x on the graph of u for all radii r up to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\alpha _0}{\Lambda }>0$$\end{document}α0Λ>0 for a constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _0>0$$\end{document}α0>0 depending only on φ .φ. The uniformity of the radii of these tangent balls allows us to estimate the deviation of the generalized normals to the reduced boundary of the subgraph of u in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I\times \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}I×R from the vertical direction (see (4.14)) which is away from 0 provided that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert u\Vert _\infty $$\end{document}‖u‖∞ is small enough depending only on φ , φ, Λ Λ and |I|. In particular, this observation and [19, Lemma 3.10] imply that u is Lipschitz continuous in I (Corollary 4.4). Finally, an explicit choice of σ σ is made, using the previous \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^\infty $$\end{document}L∞-bounds for u (Theorem 5.4). When φ φ is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2 and elliptic, the tangent φ φ-ball condition becomes equivalent to the classical tangent ball condition, and hence u must be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{1,1}$$\end{document}C1,1 in I.

Note that the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{a,b}$$\end{document}ua,b in (1.4) shows that in case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert g\Vert _\infty $$\end{document}‖g‖∞ is large, the validity of a tangent ball condition may not suffice for the regularity of minimizers of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {F}.$$\end{document}F.

When Λ =0Λ=0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma =+\infty ,$$\end{document}γ=+∞, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\gamma ,\Lambda )$$\end{document}(γ,Λ)-local minimizers coincide with classical local minimizers. In this case, assuming φ φ is symmetric with respect to the coordinate axes, we can characterize all possible Cartesian local minimizers of the φ φ-perimeter (see Theorem 3.1).

The paper is organized as follows. In Sect. 2 we introduce some preliminaries on anisotropies, Λ Λ-local minimizers, and φ φ-ball condition for Cartesian Λ Λ-local minimizers. In Sect. 3 we provide a characterization of local minimizers. Some regularity properties of Λ Λ-local minimizers and their further generalizations are studied in Sect. 4. Finally, we prove Theorem 1.2 in Sect. 5.

We dedicate this article to Umberto Massari, whose papers, as well as his book on minimal surfaces written in collaboration with M. Miranda, have been a lasting source of mathematical inspiration.

Some preliminaries

In what follows, by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}^m$$\end{document}Lm (typically for m=1,2m=1,2) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}^t$$\end{document}Ht we denote the Lebesgue measure in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^m$$\end{document}Rm and the t-dimensional Hausdorff measure in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^2.$$\end{document}R2. Depending on the context, we use |· ||·| to denote the Euclidean norm of a vector in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^2,$$\end{document}R2, the length of a bounded interval on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R and the measure of a (Lebesgue) measurable set in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^m$$\end{document}Rm for m=1,2.m=1,2. The scalar product in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}R2 is indicated by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle \cdot ,\, \cdot \right\rangle .$$\end{document}·,·. The symmetric difference of sets A and B is denoted AΔ B.AΔB. The symbol \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_r(x)$$\end{document}Br(x) stands for the Euclidean ball in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}R2 centered at x and of radius r>0.r>0. The topological closure, interior and boundary of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E\subset \mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}E⊂R2 will be denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{E},$$\end{document}E¯, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathring{E}$$\end{document}E˚ and ∂ E,∂E, respectively. Given an open interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I\subset \mathbb {R},$$\end{document}I⊂R, we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {O}(I)$$\end{document}O(I) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {O}_b(I)$$\end{document}Ob(I) to denote the collection of all open and all bounded open subsets of I, respectively.

Anisotropies

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi :\mathbb {R}^2\rightarrow [0,+\infty )$$\end{document}φ:R2→[0,+∞) be an anisotropy, i.e., a positively one-homogeneous even convex function with2.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} c\le \varphi \le \frac{1}{c} \quad \text {on the unit circle } \mathbb {S}^1 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}c≤φ≤1con the unit circleS1for some c∈ (0,1].c∈(0,1]. We denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi ^o$$\end{document}φo the dual of φ ,φ, defined as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \varphi ^o(\xi ) = \max _{\varphi (\eta )=1}\,\left\langle \xi ,\, \eta \right\rangle , $$\end{document}φo(ξ)=maxφ(η)=1ξ,η,which is also an anisotropy on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^2.$$\end{document}R2. We say that φ φ is a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^k$$\end{document}Ck-anisotropy for some k≥ 1k≥1 provided that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi \in C_{\textrm{loc}}^k(\mathbb {R}^2\setminus \{0\}).$$\end{document}φ∈Clock(R2\{0}).

The unit φ φ-ball \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^\varphi :=\{\varphi \le 1\}$$\end{document}Wφ:={φ≤1} is sometimes called the Wulff shape of φ .φ. Definition 2.1

(Elliptic anisotropy) An anisotropy φ φ on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}R2 is elliptic provided that there exists λ >0λ>0 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi (\cdot ) - \lambda |\cdot |$$\end{document}ϕ(·)-λ|·| is also an anisotropy on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^2.$$\end{document}R2.

For instance, any anisotropy induced by some positive definite quadratic form, is elliptic. The following proposition can be found in [13, Appendix A] and provides a characterization of elliptic \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2-anisotropies.

Another interesting class of anisotropies is introduced in [4, Sect. 4]:

Definition 2.3

We say an anisotropy φ φ is partially monotone if\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ (x_1,y_1),(x_2,y_2)\in \mathbb {R}^2,\quad |x_1|\le |x_2|,\,\,\,|y_1|\le |y_2|\qquad \Longrightarrow \quad \varphi (x_1,y_1)\le \varphi (x_2,y_2). $$\end{document}(x1,y1),(x2,y2)∈R2,|x1|≤|x2|,|y1|≤|y2|⟹φ(x1,y1)≤φ(x2,y2).

According to [4, Appendix A] the following statements are equivalent:φ φ is partially monotone;\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi ^o$$\end{document}φo is partially monotone;\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi (x_1,x_2)=\varphi (|x_1|,|x_2|)$$\end{document}φ(x1,x2)=φ(|x1|,|x2|) for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_1,x_2\in \mathbb {R}.$$\end{document}x1,x2∈R.Thus, φ φ is partially monotone if and only if it is even in each coordinate separately. Equivalently, φ φ is partially monotone if and only if its Wulff shape \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^\varphi $$\end{document}Wφ is symmetric with respect to the coordinate axes.

φ φ is partially monotone;

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi ^o$$\end{document}φo is partially monotone;

Anisotropic total variation and perimeter

A measurable set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E\subset \mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}E⊂R2 is called of locally finite perimeter in an open set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega \subseteq \mathbb {R}^2,$$\end{document}Ω⊆R2, and denoted as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E\in BV_{\textrm{loc}}(\Omega ;\{0,1\}),$$\end{document}E∈BVloc(Ω;{0,1}), provided that the distributional derivative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D\chi _E$$\end{document}DχE of its characteristic function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _E$$\end{document}χE is a Radon measure in Ω .Ω. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _E\in BV(\mathbb {R}^2),$$\end{document}χE∈BV(R2), we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E\in BV(\mathbb {R}^2;\{0,1\}).$$\end{document}E∈BV(R2;{0,1}). We refer for instance to [1, 11, 15, 17] for more information on BV-functions and sets of finite perimeter.

We define the φ φ-perimeter of E in the open set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega \subseteq \mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}Ω⊆R2 as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ P_\varphi (E,\Omega ) = \int _{\Omega \cap \partial ^*E} \varphi ^o(\nu _E)d\mathcal {H}^1. $$\end{document}Pφ(E,Ω)=∫Ω∩∂∗Eφo(νE)dH1.We also set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P(E,\Omega ):= P_{|\cdot |}(E,\Omega )$$\end{document}P(E,Ω):=P|·|(E,Ω) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_\varphi (E):=P_\varphi (E,\mathbb {R}^2)$$\end{document}Pφ(E):=Pφ(E,R2)

For a function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u:I\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}u:I→R we write\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textrm{sg}(u):=\{(s,t)\in I\times \mathbb {R}:\,\, \,u(s)>t\}\subset \mathbb {R}^2 $$\end{document}sg(u):={(s,t)∈I×R:u(s)>t}⊂R2to denote the (strict) subgraph of u (sometimes called hypograph). There is a natural connection between the anisotropic area of the graph and the anisotropic perimeter of the subgraph.

Lemma 2.4

[6] \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in BV_{\textrm{loc}}(I)$$\end{document}u∈BVloc(I) if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{sg}(u)$$\end{document}sg(u) has locally finite perimeter in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I\times \mathbb {R}.$$\end{document}I×R. Remark 2.6

If I is bounded and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in L^\infty (I)\cap BV_{\textrm{loc}}(I)$$\end{document}u∈L∞(I)∩BVloc(I) is a Λ Λ-local minimizer of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}_\varphi $$\end{document}Aφ in I, then u∈ BV(I).u∈BV(I). Indeed, for any open interval J⋐ IJ⋐I consider the test function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v=u\chi _{I\setminus J}.$$\end{document}v=uχI\J. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathscr {A}_\varphi (u,J) \le \varphi (\textbf{e}_2)|J| + 4\Vert u\Vert _\infty + 2\Lambda \Vert u\Vert _\infty |J|. $$\end{document}Aφ(u,J)≤φ(e2)|J|+4‖u‖∞+2Λ‖u‖∞|J|.Now letting J↗ IJ↗I we find \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}_\varphi (u,I)<+\infty $$\end{document}Aφ(u,I)<+∞ and hence u∈ BV(I).u∈BV(I). In particular, the traces of u on ∂ I∂I are well-defined.

These two notions are linked as follows.

At the moment we do not have any explicit example showing the necessity of partial monotonicity in the second assertion of the proposition.

Note that if φ φ is partially monotone, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}_\varphi (u,\cdot )=\mathscr {A}_\varphi (-u,\cdot ),$$\end{document}Aφ(u,·)=Aφ(-u,·), and hence u is Λ Λ-local minimizer if and only if so is -u.-u. Thus, from Proposition 2.7 we get the following corollary.

Density estimates

The proof of the next lemma is well-known in the literature (see e.g. [8, 13]) and can be proved, for instance, using the filling-in or cutting-out with balls.

Lemma 2.9

Let be given an anisotropy φ φ on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}R2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda \ge 0$$\end{document}Λ≥0 and an open set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega \subseteq \mathbb {R}^2.$$\end{document}Ω⊆R2. Then for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega '\Subset \Omega $$\end{document}Ω′⋐Ω there exist constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} From Lemma 2.9 and a covering argument we immediately deduce that every Λ Λ-local minimizer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E=E^{(1)}$$\end{document}E=E(1) in Ω Ω satisfies2.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & \partial E=\overline{\partial ^*E},\quad \mathcal {H}^1\big (\Omega '\cap (\partial E\setminus \partial ^*E) \big ) = 0 \nonumber \\ & \quad \text {and}\quad \mathcal {H}^1\big (\Omega '\cap (\overline{E}\setminus \mathring{E})\big ) <+\infty \quad \text {for any open } \Omega '\Subset \Omega . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂E=∂∗E¯,H1(Ω′∩(∂E\∂∗E))=0andH1(Ω′∩(E¯\E˚))<+∞for any openΩ′⋐Ω.In particular, possibly changing a negligible set, E can be assumed open or closed.

Remark 2.10

Any Λ Λ-local minimizer E of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_\varphi $$\end{document}Pφ in Ω Ω satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ P_\varphi (E,B_\rho (x)) \le P_\varphi (F,B_\rho (x)) + \Lambda \sqrt{\pi } \rho \sqrt{|E\Delta F|} $$\end{document}Pφ(E,Bρ(x))≤Pφ(F,Bρ(x))+Λπρ|EΔF|whenever \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \partial E,$$\end{document}x∈∂E, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_\rho (x)\Subset \Omega $$\end{document}Bρ(x)⋐Ω and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E\Delta F\Subset B_\rho (x).$$\end{document}EΔF⋐Bρ(x). Thus, E is ω ω-minimal in the sense of [20] with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega (\rho )=\Lambda \sqrt{\pi }\rho .$$\end{document}ω(ρ)=Λπρ. In particular, by [20, Theorem 3.4], the set Σ Σ of all points \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \Omega \cap \partial E$$\end{document}x∈Ω∩∂E around which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega \cap \partial E$$\end{document}Ω∩∂E is not a Lipschitz graph is discrete and is empty if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^\varphi $$\end{document}Wφ is not a quadrilateral1.

Classification of local minimizers

In this section we classify the minimizers of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}_\varphi ,$$\end{document}Aφ, i.e., study functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in BV_{\textrm{loc}}(I)$$\end{document}u∈BVloc(I) satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathscr {A}_\varphi (u,J)\le \mathscr {A}_\varphi (v,J) $$\end{document}Aφ(u,J)≤Aφ(v,J) for any open set J⋐ IJ⋐I and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in BV_{\textrm{loc}}(I)$$\end{document}v∈BVloc(I) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathrm {supp\,}(u-v)\Subset J.$$\end{document}supp(u-v)⋐J.

Theorem 3.1

(Characterization of local minimizers) Let φ φ be a partially monotone anisotropy, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I\subseteq \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}I⊆R be an interval and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in BV_{\textrm{loc}}(I).$$\end{document}u∈BVloc(I). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _u:=(I\times \mathbb {R})\cap \overline{\partial ^*\textrm{sg}(u)}$$\end{document}Γu:=(I×R)∩∂∗sg(u)¯ be the generalized graph of u and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu _{\textrm{sg}(u)}:\Gamma _u\rightarrow \mathbb {S}^1$$\end{document}νsg(u):Γu→S1 be the unit normal field, outer to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(I\times \mathbb {R})\cap \textrm{sg}(u),$$\end{document}(I×R)∩sg(u), defined \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}^1$$\end{document}H1-a.e. on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _u.$$\end{document}Γu. Then u is a local minimizer of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}_\varphi $$\end{document}Aφ in I if and only if there exists a vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\in \mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}N∈R2 such that3.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \varphi (N)=1\qquad \text {and}\qquad \left\langle N,\, \nu _{\textrm{sg}(u)}\right\rangle = \varphi ^o(\nu _{\textrm{sg}(u)})\quad \mathcal {H}^1 \text {-a.e. on } \Gamma _u. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}φ(N)=1andN,νsg(u)=φo(νsg(u))H1-a.e. onΓu.Moreover, u is monotone in I.

In the literature the vector N satisfying (3.1) is sometimes called a Cahn-Hoffman vector field associated to the rectifiable curve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _u.$$\end{document}Γu.

Proof

We expect this result to be well-known in the literature; for completeness we provide the proof.

⇐ ⇐ We apply a calibration argument as in [4, Example 2.4]. Assume that there exists a vector N satisfying (3.1) and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F\in BV_{\textrm{loc}}(I\times \mathbb {R};\{0,1\})$$\end{document}F∈BVloc(I×R;{0,1}) be such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F\Delta \textrm{sg}(u)\Subset J\times \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}FΔsg(u)⋐J×R for some J⋐ I.J⋐I. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P_\varphi (F,J\times \mathbb {R})&= \int _{(J\times \mathbb {R})\cap \partial ^*F} \varphi ^o(\nu _F)d\mathcal {H}^1 \ge \int _{(J\times \mathbb {R})\cap \partial ^*F} \left\langle \nu _F,\, N\right\rangle \,d\mathcal {H}^1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Pφ(F,J×R)=∫(J×R)∩∂∗Fφo(νF)dH1≥∫(J×R)∩∂∗FνF,NdH1.On the other hand, by the divergence theorem\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 0= & \int _{F\setminus \textrm{sg}(u)} \textrm{div}N\,dx- \int _{\textrm{sg}(u)\setminus F} \textrm{div}N\,dx\\= & \int _{(J\times \mathbb {R})\cap \partial ^*F} \left\langle \nu _F,\, N\right\rangle \,d\mathcal {H}^1 - \int _{(J\times \mathbb {R})\cap \partial ^*\textrm{sg}(u)} \left\langle \nu _{\textrm{sg}(u)},\, N\right\rangle \,d\mathcal {H}^1, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}0=∫F\sg(u)divNdx-∫sg(u)\FdivNdx=∫(J×R)∩∂∗FνF,NdH1-∫(J×R)∩∂∗sg(u)νsg(u),NdH1,and thus\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\int _{(J\times \mathbb {R})\cap \partial ^*F} \left\langle \nu _F,\, N\right\rangle \,d\mathcal {H}^1 =\int _{(J\times \mathbb {R})\cap \partial ^*\textrm{sg}(u)} \left\langle \nu _{\textrm{sg}(u)},\, N\right\rangle \,d\mathcal {H}^1 \\&= \int _{(J\times \mathbb {R})\cap \partial ^*\textrm{sg}(u)} \varphi ^o(\nu _{\textrm{sg}(u)})\,d\mathcal {H}^1 =P_\varphi (\textrm{sg}(u),J\times \mathbb {R}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫(J×R)∩∂∗FνF,NdH1=∫(J×R)∩∂∗sg(u)νsg(u),NdH1=∫(J×R)∩∂∗sg(u)φo(νsg(u))dH1=Pφ(sg(u),J×R).Hence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{sg}(u)$$\end{document}sg(u) is a local minimizer of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_\varphi $$\end{document}Pφ in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I\times \mathbb {R}.$$\end{document}I×R. Then Corollary 2.8 implies that u is a local minimizer of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}_\varphi $$\end{document}Aφ in I.

⇒ ⇒ Assume that u is a local minimizer of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}_\varphi $$\end{document}Aφ in I. Since |Du|(J)<+∞ |Du|(J)<+∞ for any open interval J⋐ I,J⋐I, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in L^\infty (J).$$\end{document}u∈L∞(J). In particular, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in BV_{\textrm{loc}}(I).$$\end{document}u∈BVloc(I).

Let J⋐ IJ⋐I be any interval, whose boundary points are not on the jump set of u; such an interval exists because u has at most countably many jumps. Let v be the function such that v=uv=u in I∖ JI\J and linear in J such that the traces of u and v on ∂ J∂J coincide.

Let p and q be the endpoints of the graph of v corresponding to the endpoints of J, so that the graph is the line segment [p, q].

By the local boundedness of u, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{sg}(u)\Delta \textrm{sg}(v)\Subset I\times \mathbb {R}.$$\end{document}sg(u)Δsg(v)⋐I×R. Then by the local minimality of u and the anisotropic minimality of segments [10],\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ P_\varphi (\textrm{sg}(u),J\times \mathbb {R}) \ge P_\varphi (\textrm{sg}(v),J\times \mathbb {R}) \ge P_\varphi (\textrm{sg}(u),J\times \mathbb {R}), $$\end{document}Pφ(sg(u),J×R)≥Pφ(sg(v),J×R)≥Pφ(sg(u),J×R),and hence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_\varphi (\textrm{sg}(u),J\times \mathbb {R}) = P_\varphi (\textrm{sg}(v),J\times \mathbb {R}).$$\end{document}Pφ(sg(u),J×R)=Pφ(sg(v),J×R). Choose a vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\in \mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}N∈R2 satisfying φ (N)=1φ(N)=1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle \nu _{[p,q]},\, N\right\rangle =\varphi ^o(\nu _{[p,q]})$$\end{document}ν[p,q],N=φo(ν[p,q]) on [p, q]. As above, by the divergence formula\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 0= & \int _{\textrm{sg}(v)\setminus \textrm{sg}(u)} \textrm{div}N\,dx- \int _{\textrm{sg}(u)\setminus \textrm{sg}(v)} \textrm{div}N\,dx\\ \\ = & \int _{(J\times \mathbb {R})\cap \partial ^*\textrm{sg}(v)} \left\langle \nu _F,\, N\right\rangle \,d\mathcal {H}^1 - \int _{(J\times \mathbb {R})\cap \partial ^*\textrm{sg}(u)} \left\langle \nu _{\textrm{sg}(u)},\, N\right\rangle \,d\mathcal {H}^1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}0=∫sg(v)\sg(u)divNdx-∫sg(u)\sg(v)divNdx=∫(J×R)∩∂∗sg(v)νF,NdH1-∫(J×R)∩∂∗sg(u)νsg(u),NdH1.Thus,3.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & P_\varphi (\textrm{sg}(v),J\times \mathbb {R}) \nonumber \\ & \quad = P_\varphi (\textrm{sg}(u),J\times \mathbb {R}) = \int _{(J\times \mathbb {R})\cap \partial ^*\textrm{sg}(u)} \varphi ^o(\nu _{\textrm{sg}(u)})d\mathcal {H}^1 \ge \int _{(J\times \mathbb {R})\cap \partial ^*\textrm{sg}(u)} \left\langle \nu _F,\, N\right\rangle \,d\mathcal {H}^1 \nonumber \\ & \quad =\int _{(J\times \mathbb {R})\cap \partial ^*\textrm{sg}(v)} \left\langle \nu _{\textrm{sg}(v)},\, N\right\rangle \,d\mathcal {H}^1 = \int _{(J\times \mathbb {R})\cap \partial ^*\textrm{sg}(v)} \varphi ^o(\nu _{\textrm{sg}(v)})\,d\mathcal {H}^1 =P_\varphi (\textrm{sg}(v),J\times \mathbb {R}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Pφ(sg(v),J×R)=Pφ(sg(u),J×R)=∫(J×R)∩∂∗sg(u)φo(νsg(u))dH1≥∫(J×R)∩∂∗sg(u)νF,NdH1=∫(J×R)∩∂∗sg(v)νsg(v),NdH1=∫(J×R)∩∂∗sg(v)φo(νsg(v))dH1=Pφ(sg(v),J×R),where in the fourth equality we used that v is linear in J. Thus, all inequalities in (3.2) are in fact equalities. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi ^o(\nu _{\textrm{sg}(u)}) \ge \left\langle \nu _{\textrm{sg}(u)},\, N\right\rangle $$\end{document}φo(νsg(u))≥νsg(u),N \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}^1$$\end{document}H1-a.e. on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(J\times \mathbb {R})\cap \partial ^*\textrm{sg}(u)$$\end{document}(J×R)∩∂∗sg(u) and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \int _{(J\times \mathbb {R})\cap \partial ^*\textrm{sg}(u)} \varphi ^o(\nu _{\textrm{sg}(u)})d\mathcal $$\end{document}∫(J×R)∩∂∗sg(u)φo(νsg(u))dH1=∫(J×R)∩∂∗sg(u)νsg(u),NdH1,from the Chebyshev inequality it follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi ^o(\nu _{\textrm{sg}(u)}) = \left\langle \nu _{\textrm{sg}(u)},\, N\right\rangle $$\end{document}φo(νsg(u))=νsg(u),N \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}^1$$\end{document}H1-a.e. on Example 3.2

(Strictly convex anisotropies) Assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi ^o$$\end{document}φo is strictly convex, i.e.,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \varphi ^o(x+y)<\varphi ^o(x)+\varphi ^o(y)\quad \text {whenever } |x|=|y| \text { with } x\ne \pm y. $$\end{document}φo(x+y)<φo(x)+φo(y)whenever|x|=|y|withx≠±y.Then for any interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I\subseteq \mathbb {R},$$\end{document}I⊆R, the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in BV_{\textrm{loc}}(I)$$\end{document}u∈BVloc(I) is a local minimizer of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}_\varphi $$\end{document}Aφ if and only if u is linear. Example 3.3

Example 3.4

Regularity of Λ Λ-minimizers

Now consider the case Λ >0Λ>0. In this case a general characterization of Λ Λ-local minimizers as in Theorem 3.1 seems not available. In this section, under some assumptions of φ ,φ, we show that if the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^\infty $$\end{document}L∞-norm of a Λ Λ-minimizer of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}_\varphi $$\end{document}Aφ in I is sufficiently small, then u is Lipschitz continuous in I.

Theorem 4.1

(Regularity of Λ Λ-minimizers) Let φ φ be a partially monotone anisotropy such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^\varphi $$\end{document}Wφ does not have vertical facets (so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pm \textbf{e}_1$$\end{document}±e1 is an “outer normal” to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^\varphi $$\end{document}Wφ only at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\pm \textbf{e}_1}{\varphi (\textbf{e}_1)}$$\end{document}±e1φ(e1)). Given a bounded open interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I\subset \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}I⊂R and Λ >0,Λ>0, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in BV_{\textrm{loc}}(I)$$\end{document}u∈BVloc(I) be a Λ Λ-local minimizer of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}_\varphi $$\end{document}Aφ in I satisfying4.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert u\Vert _\infty < \min \Big \{\tfrac{\alpha _0\varphi (\textbf{e}_1)}{4\Lambda }, \tfrac{\alpha _0}{2\Lambda \varphi (\textbf{e}_2)}, \, \tfrac{|I|\varphi (\textbf{e}_1)}{4\Lambda \varphi (\textbf{e}_2)}\Big \}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖u‖∞<min{α0φ(e1)4Λ,α02Λφ(e2),|I|φ(e1)4Λφ(e2)},with4.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \alpha _0=\alpha _0(\varphi ):=\tfrac{P_\varphi (W^\varphi )}{(2P_\varphi (W^\varphi )+ 1) \,\sqrt{|W^\varphi |} }>0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}α0=α0(φ):=Pφ(Wφ)(2Pφ(Wφ)+1)|Wφ|>0.Then u is Lipschitz continuous in I. Moreover, if φ φ is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2 and elliptic, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in C^{1,1}(I),$$\end{document}u∈C1,1(I), that is, u is continuously differentiable and its derivative u'u′ is Lipschitz continuous in I.

Note that every partially monotone elliptic anisotropy satisfies the assumption of the theorem. Moreover, by Remark 2.6, u∈ BV(I).u∈BV(I). Furthermore, by the partial monotonicity of φ φ and Corollary 2.8, the subgraph and epigraph of u are Λ Λ-local minimizers of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_\varphi $$\end{document}Pφ in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I\times \mathbb {R}.$$\end{document}I×R. Notice also that when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^\varphi $$\end{document}Wφ is not a quadrilateral, Remark 2.10 ensures that the boundaries of these sets in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I\times \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}I×R are locally given by a Lipschitz graph. However, this graphicality property of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{sg}(u)$$\end{document}sg(u) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{epi}(u)$$\end{document}epi(u) does not yield that u itself is Lipschitz; for instance, u may have jump discontinuities (see the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{a,b}$$\end{document}ua,b in (1.4)).

The proof of Theorem 4.1 is postponed to the end of the section after some ancillary results. We start with the following property of Wulff shapes.

Lemma 4.2

Let a bounded set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D\in BV(\mathbb {R}^2;\{0,1\})$$\end{document}D∈BV(R2;{0,1}) and a Wulff shape \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_r^\varphi $$\end{document}Wrφ with r>0r>0 be such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathring{W}_r^\varphi \cap D=\emptyset $$\end{document}W˚rφ∩D=∅ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}^1((\partial ^* D)\cap \partial W_r^\varphi )>0.$$\end{document}H1((∂∗D)∩∂Wrφ)>0. Proof

The proof is similar to [10, Proposition 6.3]. Proof

(a) Assume by contradiction that there exist \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r\in (0,\frac{\alpha _0}{\Lambda })$$\end{document}r∈(0,α0Λ) and a φ φ-ball \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_r^\varphi (y)$$\end{document}Wrφ(y) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathring{W}_r^\varphi (y)\cap F_u=\emptyset $$\end{document}W˚rφ(y)∩Fu=∅ and the intersection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\Gamma }_u \cap \partial \mathring{W}_r^\varphi (y)$$\end{document}Γ¯u∩∂W˚rφ(y) is not connected.
